Bernstein Autonomous LLP / BSG France hold a net long-or-short ≥0.5% in Core Scientific; and certain Bernstein affiliates act as market maker or liquidity provider in the equity of Cipher and TeraWulf and in the debt of Riot Platforms.** So the analyst is personally long the asset class underpinning the cohort's legacy business, and the firm carries balance-sheet positions in four of the seven names it rates Outperform. (Bernstein · disclosure appendix, 2026-08-04) [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Bernstein_WULF_AI_Inframiners_Texas_data_center_audit_to_drive_more_M.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-27) — MORGAN STANLEY SIZES THE US POWER SHORTFALL AT 38GW THROUGH 2028 (122GW THROUGH 2030), SHOWS THAT EVEN EVERY WORKAROUND WORKING LEAVES A GAP, AND PRICES THE "POWERED SHELL" CONTRACT — WHICH IS THE MOST TRANSFERABLE PIECE OF THE NOTE (Morgan Stanley "Playing the AI Infrastructure Dip", Byrd/Arcaro/Kad/Laetsch et al, 2026-07-27).**
  - **THE SHORTFALL, BUILT FROM THREE LINES:** **68GW** of US data-center power needed 2026-28, less **15GW** of data centers already under construction, less **15GW** of available/contracted grid capacity = **a 38GW potential shortfall**, growing to **~122GW through 2030**. Grid interconnection is already at **5-7 year wait times in some regions**. MS's framing of the whole People/Power/Politics problem is deliberately measured: "**we see these issues more as speed bumps than barriers**" — but "**these limitations may result in a US shortfall of data center capacity that could exceed 10% by 2028**" relative to what its semiconductor analysts' AI-chip sales forecasts imply.
  - **THE FOUR "TIME TO POWER" WORKAROUNDS, PROBABILITY-WEIGHTED — AND THEY STILL DON'T CLOSE IT.** Nat-gas turbines **15/18/20GW (low/mid/high, 90% probability of success)**; Bloom Energy fuel cells **5/7/8GW (90%)**; siting a data center at an operational nuclear plant **0/3/5GW (75%)**; converting Bitcoin sites **10/14/19GW (90%)**. Probability-weighted total **27/37/46GW**, leaving a **net shortfall of 11GW (low) / 1GW (midpoint) / zero (high)** through 2028 — **16% / 2% / 0% of 2026-28 US DC deployments.** MS marks the midpoint "the most likely outcome," so the base case is that the gap very nearly closes but only because every workaround lands.
  - **★ THE POWERED-SHELL ECONOMICS, AND WHY MS THINKS THE MARKET HAS THEM WRONG.** The comparison is to renewable PPAs, and the gap is startling. A renewable project: **$1.50-2/watt to build, ~10% unlevered FCF yield, 80%+ project leverage → at 15x EV/EBITDA, ~$1.00/watt of net value creation on a $2/watt project (~50% net, >200% on the equity portion), or ~$1,000mn per gigawatt.** A data-center powered shell: **$10-12/watt to build, 15-19% unlevered FCF yield on 15-25 year fixed-price contracts with escalators → at a 17% yield, net value creation of $15.50/watt (155% net, >700% on the equity), or ~$15,500mn per gigawatt.** MS: "**the value creation per gigawatt is ~15x higher for the powered shell relative to the renewable project.**" Across **17 actual data-center lease transactions** signed by the powered-shell cohort with AI customers, realised **value creation ranges ~$7 to $19 per watt "and continues to trend higher."**
  - **The PSP cohort and the multiple call:** **WULF, CIFR, HUT, RIOT, APLD and GLXY** "develop some of the most lucrative real estate projects in the US, but their shares declined following recent news from [[META]] and Kimi." MS believes PSPs "**will eventually trade at a 15x EV/Watt multiple, a discount to the 20-25x multiple of mature data center providers such as EQIX and DLR**," and reiterates that the market undervalues them on EV/Watt (the cohort currently spans **$2.04-$4.52 per watt**). Data-center REITs: **OW EQIX (mid-'27 PT $1,250, ~25x fwd AFFO, 15% upside), EW DLR, EW BXDC**; DLR's 2Q26 showed **record 0-1MW bookings and a $1.4bn backlog**.
  - **The developers and the IPPs:** **SEI (Solaris)** — MS values the existing **3,200MW procured fleet through 2029 at ~$66/share standalone** (with **800-900MW available and likely contracted near-term**) plus **$24/share for post-2028 growth** assuming 500MW added annually through 2035, against a **140GW data-center TAM by 2030**; **[[WMB]]** — "the broadest integrated model," combining pipeline laterals and gas supply (sometimes upstream/gathering/processing) with gas-fired generation, fiber, batteries and potentially CCS; **LBRT** — Power-as-a-Service targeting **~3GW of onsite capacity by 2029**, with **1GW committed to Vantage Data Centers including a 400MW firm reservation by 2027** and an SLB alliance for modular DC infrastructure; **BE (Bloom)** onsite fuel cells and **XE** small modular turbines/SMRs; grid-connected producers **[[CEG]], [[VST]], [[TLN]], BEP, CWEN** on the simple shortfall logic.
  - **★ POLITICS IS NOW AN UNDERWRITABLE RISK, NOT A TAIL RISK.** "Over the past decade, states competed with one another to offer generous packages to attract data centers… **We are now beginning to see a reversal, with states now moving to pause, condition, or repeal data center tax breaks.**" Utility rate freezes, ratepayer-shielding and slowing DC development "have increasingly become part of gubernatorial campaign platforms, and we would expect this to be an important voter issue in November." Federally, the **Ratepayer Protection Act** is in the House — it "would require state utilities to consider creating a **'large load standard'** requiring data centers to pay for grid upgrades," codifying the March **Ratepayer Protection Pledge** signed by **Amazon, Google, Meta, Microsoft, Oracle, xAI and a frontier lab**, and would "essentially create a national data center tariff." MS's investment conclusion from the politics: it pushes work toward **onsite generation** and toward jurisdictions that still have incentives. (Morgan Stanley, 2026-07-27) [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Morgan_Stanley_700HK_Global_Thematics_Playing_the_AI_Infrastructure_Dip_Whe.html)
  - **⚠ CROSS-CHECK AGAINST THE HYPERSCALERS' OWN 07-30 DISCLOSURES, WHICH ARE MORE OPTIMISTIC THAN MS'S SHORTFALL IMPLIES:** [[MSFT]] added **a full gigawatt for the third consecutive quarter** (31 data centers in the quarter, 88 for the year) and reaffirmed doubling capacity in two years; UBS's Keirstead reads that pace as evidence the industry is "**powering through those bottlenecks**" and says it "undermines a lot of the media stories about AI data center go-live bottlenecks." [[AMZN]] said it is "on pace" to **double power capacity by end-'27 versus '25**, with the lion's share of '27 capacity "**largely reserved**" and "quite a bit" of '28 already reserved. **Neither is inconsistent with a 38GW national shortfall — the hyperscalers are the buyers who get served first — but the page should not carry the shortfall as evidence that the largest operators are supply-blocked. They are the ones clearing the queue.** (MSFT 4QFY26 + UBS callback + AMZN Q2'26 call, 2026-07-29/30)
- **NEW (2026-07-29) — THE CONSTRUCTION LAYER GETS ITS OWN BOTTOM-UP MODEL: 61GW+ OF MODULAR CAPACITY TRACKED, THE LABOR BOTTLENECK QUANTIFIED TRADE BY TRADE, AND A HARD FEDERAL CEILING ON HOW BIG A MODULE CAN LEGALLY BE (SemiAnalysis "The Wild Wild West Of LEGO Datacenters", 2026-07-29).** A 75-page teardown of how AI datacenters are actually built, and the first work on this page that treats *construction method* — not generation, not the grid — as a driver of GW delivered. It maps a modular universe of **>80 vendors**, tracks **>61 GW of modular capacity across 1,000+ sites** in its Industrials Model, grades whether vendor "modular" claims hold up, and estimates **modular penetration reaches 30%+ of total live capacity by end-2028**.
  - **(1) LABOR IS THE BOTTLENECK "CAPITALIST INCENTIVES ALONE CANNOT BUILD PAST," AND IT IS NOW SIZED.** Electricians are **30-40% of total construction man-hours** on a datacenter project, with an **electrician shortage emerging in 2027**, most acute where the buildout concentrates — **Texas and Ohio**. Per-MW intensity: an AI data hall absorbs **~12,000 field man-hours per MW** during MEP fit-out, so a **50 MW hall concentrates ~600,000 field hours and stacks ~300 craft workers at peak**. Relocating that MEP scope to a factory cuts **on-site hours ~63% to ~4,500/MW and licensed-electrician hours ~85%**. The wage gap is *not* the lever — BLS puts **field construction at $34/hr vs $33/hr in the factory**, but overtime and site premiums lift the **effective field electrician to ~$63/hr**; the real saving is factory throughput. Field evidence already on the tape: **Crusoe pumped wages 30% to pull talent to the Abilene site, which needed 9,000+ workers at peak.**
  - **(2) THE SCHEDULE, BROKEN INTO ITS FOUR BLOCKS — AND PERMITTING SITS OUTSIDE THE CLOCK EVERYONE QUOTES.** A conventional 50 MW building runs **~18-24 months groundbreaking → IT-ready**: groundworks **4-6 months (~5)**, structure and shell **2.5-4.5 (~3.5)**, mechanical-and-electrical fit-out **7-11**, commissioning to IT-ready **3-8 (single-hall midpoint ~4.5)**. **Permitting adds another 12-13 months that cannot overlap construction**, taking the all-in to **~30-35+ months for a stick build vs ~24-30 for modular**. Full modular compresses the construction window to **~12-18 months — ~36% faster than a pure stick build and ~30% faster than today's partly-modular baseline**; MEP-skids-only lands **~17 months**; all-in-one prefab blocks or containerized DCs can get to **~12 months**. SemiAnalysis flags that aggressive "6-9 month" claims are partly a **counting convention** (starting the clock at electrical fit-out or vertical works rather than groundworks) and partly **compressed commissioning**, which "significantly shifts risk into operations."
  - **(3) SPEED IS WORTH MORE THAN THE CAPEX SAVING, AND THE $/MW/MONTH NUMBERS ARE THE USEFUL PART.** Capex savings are real but small: full modular on a 50 MW liquid-cooled US hall costs **~$1.1mn/MW less all-in — ~$13.5mn vs ~$14.6mn/MW, just under ~8%** (moving MEP to the factory saves ~$0.6mn/MW of construction services and ~$0.5mn/MW of installation). The **schedule** is where the money is: a megawatt of IT load generates **~$12-15mn of revenue a year, ~$1-1.25mn per IT MW per month** for a CSP (and "Companies like Anthropic or OpenAI are making over **$50mn/MW on API**"). On the cost side, **GPU depreciation averages ~$30mn/MW over five years ≈ ~$500k/MW/month**, stranded whether or not the hall is ready — so SemiAnalysis values an earlier month at **~$500k/MW to an owner-operator** and only **~$190k/MW/month (~$190/kW/mo) to a wholesale colo that never owns the GPUs**. On an ~8-month lead that is **~$200mn undiscounted across a 50 MW hall, ~$4mn/MW**. **The governing caveat, which belongs on this page more than anywhere: "go-live is the latest of three dates, building ready, power available, and GPUs delivered, and accelerating the building earns nothing if it was not the date that bound."** Against the savings, the largest structural penalty is a **double margin** — a module-vendor margin stacked on the site integrator's — which compresses only where the OEM also integrates on site (Vertiv, Schneider on turnkey programs); Schneider's own WP163 puts **module hardware ~40% above traditional, netting to 13% lower first cost** after design and install-labor savings.
  - **(4) ⚠️ THE UNDERRATED STRUCTURAL CONSTRAINT: US FEDERAL LAW CAPS THE MODULE. "Federal law fixes the no-permit envelope at 102 inches wide and 80,000 pounds gross, leaving about 24 tons for the module on a standard deck."** Designs that ride inside a **40-foot ISO container at 96 inches wide** — Microsoft's Azure Modular Datacenter and Schneider's Easy Modular are the two examples given — **cross any state line or fly in a C-17 permit-free**. Past that you need a permit, and **the cost is trivial while the schedule is not**: an oversize permit runs **$15-100 a state** and line-haul **$12-14 a loaded mile** (a 500-mile move ≈ **$6-7k a trailer**), but **loads past roughly 16 feet become superloads, triggering a bridge-engineering review of 7-21 days per state that "stacks toward months across a route,"** plus escorted restricted travel windows. Thresholds are **not uniform** — a module engineered to clear **Virginia (superload at 18 ft or 250,000 lb) can still trip Ohio's 14-ft / 120,000-lb line**. Craning is the second physical gate: a **Manitowoc 18000 lifts ~600 tons at 7.3 m but only ~10 tons at 104 m**, with lifts at **~$5,000-25,000/day**; a single **Schneider 500 kW power module weighs 50,000 lb and needs six lifting points**. Insurance binds too — **high-value AI racks may ship only one or two at a time**. Operators are designing around the haul: **AWS engineers Houdini's skids onto low double-drop trailers to stay under bridges** ([AMZN](../AMZN.md)), **Nautilus barged a datacenter 50 miles to the Port of Stockton**, and **Compass put a Schneider module factory next to its Red Oak campus**. This is a hard ceiling on module size that shapes who can win — and it is why the ISO-container form factor persists despite its density penalty.
  - **(5) COMMISSIONING IS THE GAP NOBODY HAS CLOSED — AND IT IS ON-SITE BY PHYSICS.** Full site commissioning runs **3 to 8 months end to end**, and operators "consistently call this the single biggest gap in the modular cycle." The industry runs a 6-level ladder (L1 factory witness "red tag" → L5 integrated systems testing "white tag"), and **from L2 onward the work must happen physically on site, because the sources being tested against — the utility feed, the generators, the BESS — only exist there.** The binding constraint at scale is **L3 point-to-point** work (every PDU, control panel and field device wired, addressed, named and verified back to the BMS, across hundreds of PDUs each with dozens-to-hundreds of points). Two recoverable levers: **parallelize L2-L4 on early modules against later deliveries so only L5 waits for the full set**, and **use the plant as its own load bank** (the power equipment and its battery storage serve as the load bank, so the gear that proves the plant stays as part of it, instead of trucking in rented gensets and resistive banks).
  - **(6) WHO OWNS THE MODULARIZATION — THREE MODELS, AND THE CAPACITY IS SOLD OUT IN ALL OF THEM.** **Operator-led** (spec + owner-furnished gear, integrator assembles only) is "effectively confined to the largest hyperscalers" because it means carrying all cost, inventory and lead-time risk without a vendor's allocation leverage — **AWS's Project Houdini** and **Aligned** are the examples. **EPC/integrator-led** is vendor-agnostic and the seat hyperscalers prefer, since they already know the equipment they want: **Comfort Systems (FIX), Sterling Infrastructure (STRL), Quanta's Cupertino Electric** ([PWR](../PWR.md)), plus specialists **PCX, Nautilus, DXN, Infra Partners, Bladeroom**. **OEM-led** is **Vertiv OneCore** ([VRT](../VRT.md)) — highest content capture (**~$3.5mn/MW discrete → ~$7mn/MW full stack**) but capacity-limited: **Vertiv's modular lead times run over 12 months**, and "this is also pushing big OEMs, not only Vertiv but also companies like **Schneider and Siemens**, to be selective with capacity slot allocation, requiring certain capacity minimums and favoring bigger projects" — which routes smaller developers to the integrators. Upstream, **switchgear and transformers still carry 12-18-month lead times** and customization "can reopen engineering and add roughly eight weeks."
  - **(7) SCHNEIDER ELECTRIC IS THE SECOND-BIGGEST NAME IN THIS NOTE AND HAS NO WIKI PAGE — the marks worth carrying.** Schneider (SU FP) has "the second-broadest modular portfolio after Vertiv, spanning nearly the full modular taxonomy." **Secure Power Systems (three-phase UPS + prefabricated datacenter products) is almost 32% of Schneider's Data Center & Networks portfolio.** Architecturally it is the opposite of OneCore: **EcoStruxure is a family of configurable building blocks** customers deploy independently or combine around their own redundancy design, where **OneCore is sold as a coordinated above-foundation delivery system**. Its **EcoStruxure Pod** white-space product takes **up to 40 racks** and Schneider "works with Nvidia to support more than 30+ reference designs." Commercially: a **$3bn multi-year agreement with Compass** covering prefabricated infrastructure, backed by a **105,000 sq ft integration facility beside Compass's Red Oak campus**, with Schneider manufacturing and testing the power and white-space systems while Compass builds the shell in parallel. **Management estimates Schneider's addressable datacenter content at ~$1.2mn to $3.3mn per MW; group backlog now exceeds €25bn with 18-24 months of visibility across datacenter projects.** The stated limitation: dependence on operator/integrator partnerships to assemble the full facility.
  - **(8) THE PUBLIC BENEFICIARY SET AND THE PRIVATE CHALLENGERS.** SemiAnalysis names the public beneficiaries as **FIX, STRL, PWR, VRT, SU, FLEX** — i.e. two of them are on this wiki as pages ([PWR](../PWR.md), [VRT](../VRT.md), [FLEX](../FLEX.md)), two are US-listed contractors with no page (**Comfort Systems (FIX)**, **Sterling Infrastructure (STRL)**), and one is European (**Schneider Electric**). Because **big OEMs are sold out and top EPC/integrator capacity is locked up by hyperscalers**, private challengers are entering with real nameplate: **InfraPartners** ("the name we hear more often" — delivered Nscale's Glomfjord facility in Norway, billed as the first fully modular DC purpose-built for AI, disclosed **~1.2 GW of annual deployment capacity**, ships out of a **150,000 sq ft Houston plant** with its Romania factory doubling footprint, launched the productized **RapidNode / RapidHub / RapidFrame** suite in March 2026, and is stacking into the power chain via **DG Matrix, Nvidia, EPRI and Prologis** partnerships); **BladeRoom** (UK BRG Technologies; **30+ datacenters delivered across Europe/Asia/Africa in as little as 20 weeks**, now offering **30MW-plus hyperscale blocks**, US entry via a JV with **Rosendin's Modular Power Solutions**, manufacturing in Michigan); **Faith Technologies (FTI)** — "a pure play on the electrical labor bottleneck," shipping complete modular electrical buildings via its **Excellerate** brand, with a **438,000 sq ft Olathe flagship** plus a **$79mn Alabama plant, a 500,000 sq ft Indiana facility and further El Paso capacity — roughly tripling its footprint**; plus **Cadolto** and **Flexnode** (the latter the private modular-datahall vendor partnered with Eaton — **not** Flex Ltd). Other sized marks from the same vendor walk, none with wiki pages: **Comfort Systems** — modular **17% of 1H26 revenue (~$2bn annualized)**, record backlog **$14.1bn (+73% y/y)** with modular ~a third of the sequential increase, **$1.14bn of quarterly operating cash flow** on advance billings up **$1.11bn over six months to $3.23bn**, CY26 modular capex alone greater than the cumulative modular capex of the preceding 12 years (taking modular production space to **5m sq ft by late summer 2027**), and newly disclosed **pilot modular contracts with frontier AI labs and colocation providers**; **Sterling Infrastructure** — addressable datacenter capacity expanding **~3.8 GW → 14 GW** on the Texas/Pacific-Northwest/Midwest pull, a Texas lease roughly tripling modular capacity with throughput about doubled; **EMCOR** — ~**35,000 tradespeople**, ~**95% of fabrication for its own projects**, adding **400-450k sq ft over 18 months**, performance obligation **$4.5bn vs $4.1bn of 2025 revenue**, and AI datacenters carrying **~1.2x the electrical and 1.4-1.8x the mechanical content** of traditional cloud facilities; **ABB** — HiPerGuard at **2.5 MVA per unit combinable into 25 MW blocks**, a **34.5 kV version shrinking the electrical footprint ~20-25%**, deployed at **Applied Digital's 400 MW North Dakota campus**, a VoltaGrid partnership whose first two phases cover **62 synchronous condensers**, **electrification orders +60% in Q2 2026 to $7.2bn with $13.7bn backlog**, and ~**$200mn** of European MV capacity being added; **Siemens** — Smart Infrastructure datacenter revenue **~€2.9bn in FY2025 (+40%)**, DC orders **€1.8bn Q1 FY26 / €1.9bn Q2**, and a program for **up to 1,500 modular switchgear skids over five years** for a colo developer SemiAnalysis believes is Compass; **Modine** — a fully captured cooling package **~$0.6mn/MW rising toward ~$0.7mn/MW**, plus **>$4bn of cooling capacity reserved for a hyperscale customer 2027-2029 with a $165mn upfront payment** (suspected Google); **Jacobs Solutions** — traditional design scope **~$2-5mn for a 50 MW-class facility** against current full-program EPCM engagements management says are "two orders of magnitude larger"; **Foxconn** — **>1 GW of deployed modular DC infrastructure**, ~**1,000 AI racks/week** scaling toward **2,000** at a 242,000 sq ft Houston facility plus a **$569mn Wisconsin expansion**.
  - **(9) THE OPERATOR PLAYBOOKS, WITH THE NUMBERS.** **Compass** is the longest-running modular colo: **~70-85% of each building manufactured off-site**, framework and roof standing in **18-21 days**, a rebar-free fiber-reinforced precast shell cast from on-site batch plants, **Schneider EcoStruxure Pods** in white space, a repeatable **~1.25 MW Schneider power center** (Galaxy VX UPS, lithium-ion, QED-2 switchgear), and Siemens MV switchgear skids — run across **up to 360 MW at Red Oak, Texas** and **eight buildings / 1.8m sq ft at Goodyear, Arizona**. **QTS** locks design early and pre-buys long-lead gear, holding **~7m sq ft of Kansas warehouse capacity** for UPS/switchgear/cooling inventory; its Freedom design pairs a **1.5 MW UPS + switchgear package with a 2.25 MW generator scaling in 1.5 MW increments**, Freedom LC+ extends it to air-or-liquid cooling, and its newer rapid-deployment design organizes capacity around **repeatable 60 MW data-hall blocks** (the "Rapids" design, **adopted at scale by two major AI companies**). **QTS estimates labor pressure has increased datacenter construction costs by roughly 20-30% per MW** — a clean, quotable inflation mark for this page. Its **Cedar Rapids** campus shows the ceiling: a **420 MW phase spanning ~2.8m sq ft on ~28,000 tons of structural steel, groundbreaking to topping out in ~5 months and the broader building delivered in ~11 months**. **Aligned** standardizes power/cooling while letting the hall flex — a **2 MW UPS container**, then Adaptive Modular Infrastructure (**Delta³ air to ~50 kW/rack, DeltaFlow liquid above 350 kW/rack**) — at **Project Caprock, 540 MW across six buildings and 1.65m sq ft**; it also **shipped a 3 MW module Utah→Omaha and back with force loggers** to measure transit stress, because "a module may experience greater mechanical stress on the road than during normal operation." **Crusoe** works both layers: at Abilene each building used **~672 factory-made insulated metal panels, fabricated in under 40 days and installed at ~15-20/day to reach a dried-in shell in under eight weeks**, while its 2022 **Easter-Owens** acquisition brought modular/electrical manufacturing in-house and its **Spark** factory in Brighton, Colorado ships **~1 MW substantially-complete units** — **four Spark units plus a 12 MW microgrid at Redwood Materials in Nevada, since expanded to 24 units**. **EdgeConneX** supplies the permitting arbitrage: **a common design can advance a project to roughly a 30-60% permit set before site-specific localization is complete.** On cooling infrastructure specifically, **Karman Industries'** CO2-based Heat Processing Unit ships as an outdoor-rated NEMA skid at **4-5x conventional power density and cuts yard footprint by 60-80%** — relevant to the mechanical-yard bottleneck as liquid-cooled piping loads rise.
  - **(10) THE NVIDIA LAYER — DSX AS A FACILITY-LEVEL REFERENCE DESIGN.** NVIDIA now publishes a reference design for the entire AI factory: **DSX**, first unveiled as an Omniverse digital-twin blueprint at **GTC Washington in October 2025**, formalized as the **Vera Rubin DSX reference design in March 2026**, and since expanded into the full DSX platform. It covers compute, networking, storage and cluster design *plus* the facilities side — power, cooling, controls, and even civil/structural/architectural design. Two named products matter to this page: **DSX Max-Q maximizes tokens per watt within a fixed power budget**, and **DSX Flex "facilitates the connection [of] the facility to grid services, dynamically adjusting power draw and orchestrating demand with hybrid onsite generation"** — i.e. NVIDIA is now shipping the demand-response/behind-the-meter orchestration layer as part of the reference architecture, which is the software counterpart to the BTM gas/fuel-cell buildout this page already tracks. The ecosystem spans **Cadence, Dassault Systèmes, Eaton, Jacobs, Nscale, Phaidra, Procore, PTC, Schneider Electric, Siemens, Switch, Trane and Vertiv**; **CoreWeave is already using DSX Air to build and test digital twins of its AI factories**; **Vertiv's OneCore packages power and cooling into standardized 12.5 MW pods**; and **[ETN](../ETN.md)'s "Eaton Beam Rubin DSX" combines Fibrebond enclosures, NordicEPOD power skids, Flexnode modular halls, 800 VDC distribution and Boyd liquid cooling around the Vera Rubin reference architecture** (Siemens is separately "among the first suppliers with both IEC and UL variants" for Rubin DSX). Cross-filed to [800v-dc-power](800v-dc-power.md) for the 800 VDC and SST-timeline implications; NVDA-specific DSX detail belongs on that page. (SemiAnalysis "The Wild Wild West Of LEGO Datacenters", 2026-07-29)
- **NEW (2026-07-29, note dated 07-28) — the xAI/[[SPCX]] 8GW buildout stress-tested gate by gate: power generation and permitting are the binding gates; chips, T&D and capital cleared (FUNDA channel-check deep dive, 2026-07-28).** Six independent supply-side sources converge on **3.5–5GW by YE2027 vs the 8GW of ordered compute** (13k GB300 racks '26 + 15k Rubin racks '27 on ~1.5GW existing); FUNDA's ladder: Bear 4GW ~25% / Base 5GW ~50% / Bull 8GW ~15–20% / Tail <3GW ~5–10%. The datapoints for this page: **new-turbine OEM slots are booked out to 2029–2030, so near-term increments come only from second-hand/imports/refurbs/acquisitions at 2–4x aftermarket premiums** (case in point: Fortress bought APR Energy's 38 turbines for ~$375M and flipped them to the Musk complex for **$1.9B, ~5x**); the **$1.465B Jereh × FTAI JV order** (>1GW ≈ 40–45 mobile aeroderivative 24–25MW gensets on second-hand CFM56 cores, staged to Nov-2027; Jereh provisioning ≥2GW/yr; engine cores multi-sourced Siemens/Baker Hughes/Kawasaki/FTAI) is the China-supply-chain fix in motion — and its **mobile + water-injection-NOx spec is read as purpose-built to run under temporary permits, i.e. in the regulatory seam** (SCR units take 9–14 months and aqueous-ammonia catalyst is backordered a year+; **every turbine at Colossus 1 runs unpermitted — the endgame is binary, workaround or shutdown**; PSD = 1–1.5yr review + 1.5yr build, "not achievable before 2028"). **T&D explicitly cleared** by one of xAI's own MV-transformer suppliers — "5–6GW of new xAI capacity in 2027 is realistic from a transformer and electrical-equipment standpoint" — with **mainland-Chinese vendors (Sanbian "the most deeply involved with xAI," Jinpan, Sieyuan, TBEA, China XD) already inside US datacenters and undercutting Taiwanese vendors even after tariffs** (lead-time ladder: Western 3–4yr / Taiwan 18mo–2.5yr / xAI's no-US-content MV spec 15mo / China 220kV-class 3mo); rules of thumb: **1GW campus ≈ 1,000MVA of transformer capacity; transformers + distribution ≈ 7–8% of DC capex**; through 2027 HVDC still routes medium-voltage → transformer → rectifier and **solid-state transformers have no use case before 2028** — corroborating the MSFT DC program manager's 2030–2032 SST timeline above against [[GEV]]'s "'27 and beyond" vendor framing. Basis discipline (the note is itself disciplined about this): generation nameplate (38 turbines, **1.14GW ISO, ~750MW usable in summer**) ≠ IT load (~1.29GW) ≠ facility (~1.5GW at PUE 1.15–1.2). Full scenarios + the Aug-4 checkpoints on [[SPCX]]. (FUNDA, 2026-07-28)
- **NEW (2026-07-27) — MS PUTS A HARD DOLLAR NUMBER ON THE POWERED SHELL AND THE ENERGY LINE INSIDE ONE GIGAWATT, AND NAMES ITS UTILISATION ASSUMPTION (Morgan Stanley "The Paths to 25-50% GenAI ROIC" · Brian Nowak, CFA, with Stephen Byrd and Adam Wood, Internet | North America, 2026-07-27).** ⚠️ **Proportionality note first: this is a ROIC note, not a power note. It contains no grid, generation, interconnect-queue or cooling content. Three items belong on this page and nothing beyond them is claimed.**
  - **(1) THE CAPACITY WALL, RESTATED FROM MS's 12 JUL WORK: "compute capacity expected to quadruple from '25 to '28 (reaching an estimated ~120 GW)."** Already carried on this page from the 07-12 note; **logged here only as confirmation that the 07-27 ROIC framework is built on the same 120 GW base, not as a new mark.**
  - **🔴 (2) THE PER-GW COST SPLIT, WHICH IS THE NEW PART AND IS EXACTLY THE NON-IT LINE THIS PAGE CARES ABOUT. MS anchors to "an all-in capital cost of $39bn/GW for GB300 as implied by our bottoms-up capex/capacity work," decomposed as IT CAPEX $23bn ON A 5-YEAR USEFUL LIFE and NON-IT CAPEX $16bn ON A 15-YEAR USEFUL LIFE, with "~$4mn per GB300 rack" and 410,256 GB300 chips per GW.** **The two operating lines that follow are the ones to hold: NON-IT DEPRECIATION ~$1bn/yr per GW and ENERGY + OTHER OPEX (energy, labor, maintenance) ~$2bn/yr per GW, inside a total opex of $7.6bn/GW.** **So: MS is saying the powered shell is a $16bn, 15-year asset per gigawatt, and that running it costs about $2bn a year in energy and site opex. That is the cleanest sourced sizing on this page of the non-IT half of a gigawatt — and note the shape it implies: the shell is 41% of the capital but only ~13% of the annual depreciation charge, because it is amortised over three times the life of the silicon. Anyone modelling powered-shell demand off capex dollars and anyone modelling it off P&L depreciation will get very different answers.**
  - **(3) UTILISATION, WITH A STATED BASIS: "We also assume a utilization rate of 75% on owned GPUs (in line with INDUSTRY AVERAGE FOR A WELL-OPTIMIZED AI DATA CENTER)."** **Useful precisely because it is labelled — 75% is an industry-average-for-a-good-site assumption, not a physical ceiling and not a fleet average, and it is the number MS's whole ROIC stack rests on.** MS applies the haircut to owned infrastructure but explicitly **not** to its third-party/rented framework, which is why that case shows a higher revenue per watt. The ROIC frameworks themselves sit on [tokenmaxxing.md](tokenmaxxing.md); the capex aggregation on [hyperscaler-capex.md](hyperscaler-capex.md). [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/INTERNET_20260727_2024.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-26) — the same BofA framework, restated with a bigger top-line gap and two sizing datapoints not previously on this page (BofA Global Research · Jean Ann Salisbury / Andrew Obin, "Must Read Research This Week," 07-24, received 07-26):** **"We've estimated that US electricity demand requires the equivalent of 177GW of new capacity between 2026-30, but utilities are likely to supply just 93 gigawatts (GW), leaving a gap exceeding 80GW."** That is a larger and more precisely bounded version of the ">100GW shortfall" already on this page (Obin/Arya, 07-08) — note the two are framed differently (177-vs-93 is a capacity-equivalent gap; the earlier figure was a generation shortfall), so they are complements rather than a supersession. **The two net-new sizing lines: "hyperscalers plan to spend over $5trn in the next five years, yet only ~9% of US data centers have onsite generation," and "if 100% of BTM is met by gas, we estimate the opportunity is 84GW."** The 9% figure is the useful one — it is the penetration denominator for the entire behind-the-meter thesis. **Salisbury's database is restated at 158GW of announced projects with only 30-50% "achievable by 2030," leaving a 40-80GW gap** (already on file from 07-25, unchanged). Buy-rated BTM names: **WMB, CVX, KGS, PUMP, LBRT**, with "CVX and WMB's gas supply and marketing capabilities position[ing] them well for continued share gains **even as turbine availability becomes less tight**." ⚠️ Note the source's own interest and the read-across it implies: this is BofA's **oil-and-gas** team making a case for onsite gas generation, i.e. partly **substitution away from grid-scale CCGT and from the utility interconnect queue** — so it is simultaneously a demand argument for turbines/engines and a competitive argument against the utility route. Both readings kept. See [[GEV]], [[CEG]], [[TLN]].
- **NEW (2026-07-26) — the cost governor on the cooling stack, which is the constraint nobody models (AlphaSense expert call — Principal Product Manager at AMD, 2026-07-26): "cooling infrastructure should not exceed 9-10% of total data center operating or CapEx costs, and solutions must be forward-compatible with future data center scaling."** Against that budget: **next-generation GPUs "reaching up to 2,400 W" TDP**, with **direct-to-chip liquid cooling the current standard** and the advanced alternatives (two-phase immersion, active thermal management, new TIMs, thermoelectric cooling) **"still two to three years away" from commercial deployment**, adoption gradual, and hyperscalers preferring **modular, retrofittable** solutions over architectural change. Separately on power architecture, a **Microsoft data-centre program manager expects solid-state transformers to become the 800V DC standard but "does not expect widespread adoption before 2030-2032,"** with 800V "limited to pilot deployments" today — ⚠️ **materially later than [[GEV]]'s own SST commercial framing of "'27 and beyond."** See [800v-dc-power](800v-dc-power.md) for the full vendor-vs-buyer gap.
- **NEW (2026-07-26) — the financing constraint moves from "will it be funded" to "can the bond market digest it" (The Information, "AI Financing Gets Creative," 2026-07-26):** Goldman's global head of infrastructure and real asset finance, **John Greenwood**, is "looking for capital in every nook and cranny" to support an expected **$7.5 trillion in spending on chips, data centers and power in the next five years** — and notes "the hunt won't end there, since much of that spending is on GPUs and other chips that need replacing every few years," i.e. a recurring raise on a 3-5 year clock rather than a one-time one. The panel's flagged strain: **"bond markets show 'digestion issues' after massive AI-related debt issuances."** And the equity-market version of the same signal, from Vital Knowledge: **"the tumble in SPCX shares in particular is a big red flag for the prospects of AI-linked IPOs over the coming months and without the money from those offerings, capex will inevitably slow."** See [hyperscaler-capex](hyperscaler-capex.md), [[SPCX]].
- **NEW (2026-07-25) — BofA sizes the behind-the-meter answer to its own 100GW gap, and concludes half of it doesn't get built (BofA cross-sector power team, via Nicholas Villani "US Insight Daily", 2026-07-24).** Following the prior week's cross-sector note identifying a **100GW gap between power demand and available power supply through 2030**, the team went a level deeper on **behind-the-meter (BTM) supply — "basically the 'only option' to address that gap, given all the in-front-of-the-meter stuff is spoken for and the US grid is maxxed-out."** The punchline: they found **158GW of announced BTM projects**, but believe **only 30-50% of them actually get through — leaving a residual gap of >50GW**. From the viable pool, **Chevron and Williams are expected to be the main deliverers**. This is the first quantified BTM pipeline-vs-conversion estimate on this page and it should be the working assumption behind any "announced GW" figure quoted elsewhere in the wiki: **announced ≠ delivered, and the haircut is roughly half.**
- **NEW (2026-07-25) — regulators have shifted from "demand only" to "show me they're real," and speed-to-market is now the bottleneck (BofA · Ross Fowler, NARUC 2026 Summer Policy Summit takeaways, via Villani, 2026-07-24).** Fowler attended the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners summit — the venue where regulators signal how they are thinking about the next decade of power demand and grid investment — and reports the data-centre story shifting from "**demand only**" to now "**show me they're real**": transparency around **customer commitments, infrastructure requirements, cost allocation, interconnection timing and local impacts** are becoming the key factors in whether demand translates into **approved investment and earnings growth**. His summary line: "**speed-to-market has become the critical bottleneck for AI-driven demand, and execution matters more than opportunity.**" Read together with the BTM bullet above, the regulatory gate and the physical gate point the same way — **deliverability, not appetite, is the binding constraint** — which is supportive of pricing and backlog quality for equipment and generation, and cautionary on the pace at which announced GW converts into orders. Cross-ref [../GEV.md](../GEV.md), [../VRT.md](../VRT.md), [800v-dc-power.md](800v-dc-power.md).
- **NEW (2026-07-25) — the turbine capacity-vs-demand balance, quantified: BofA sees industry demand of 115-120GW/yr in 2026-28 against expected capacity of ~102GW (BofA · Alex Jones, Siemens Energy note, via Villani, 2026-07-24).** Jones argues the fear that expanding turbine manufacturing capacity leads to oversupply is **misplaced**: while **GE Vernova plans to increase gas-turbine capacity to 30GW by 2030**, BofA forecasts **industry demand of 115-120GW annually between 2026-28, well above expected capacity of roughly 102GW** — and on top of that, **servicing a rapidly growing installed base could consume up to 25% of peak OEM capacity by 2035**, supporting a tighter-for-longer market. He sees continued **pricing power, strong order momentum and significant upside to consensus earnings**, making Siemens Energy one of his highest-conviction ideas (**€260 price objective, ~70% upside**). **The read for the theme: if the binding constraint on gas generation is manufacturing capacity rather than demand, then order-book deceleration at any single OEM is a rationing artefact, not a demand signal** — directly relevant to the "peak order demand" reaction to [../GEV.md](../GEV.md)'s print.
- **NEW (2026-07-25) — a policy datapoint on the other side: New York becomes the first US state to impose a data-centre moratorium** (DataCenterDynamics, via Susquehanna · Mehdi Hosseini's Technology Hardware weekly, 2026-07-25). First state-level moratorium logged on this page. Small in isolation — New York is not a primary AI-datacenter geography — but it is the concrete instance of the "local impacts" factor Fowler flags as an emerging regulatory gate in the NARUC bullet above, and worth tracking as a template other states may follow.

- ⚠️ **NEW (2026-07-24) — the hard rack-power comparison, and it CONTRADICTS the prevailing "AMD is less power-hungry" view: AMD MI455X Helios at ~240kW server / ~257kW all-in per rack vs GB300 NVL72's ~142kW / ~159kW (SemiAnalysis "Can AMD break the CUDA Moat? AMD Advancing AI 2026", 2026-07-24).** The single most consequential power datapoint of the batch for anyone modelling GW-per-rack density. SemiAnalysis: *"For operating cost of ownership, MI455X lands even beyond VR NVL72 in power draw, reaching close to ~240kW of server power consumption and ~257kW of all-in power consumption per server, as compared to GB300 NVL72's ~142kW of server power consumption and ~159kW of all-in power consumption per server."* **This directly disagrees with the widely-held sell-side view that AMD platforms are less power-hungry than Nvidia's** — BofA's "Watts to Tokens" (Arya, 2026-05-25) concluded on its rack-power-budget analysis that AMD's platform is **"less power-hungry than Nvidia's"** (that mark sits on [../AMD.md](../AMD.md)). **Both are logged here, attributed, with the disagreement flagged rather than resolved:** they are not measuring the same thing — BofA works from marketed platform power budgets, SemiAnalysis from a bottoms-up Helios rack build (which also carries the copper/retimer overhead: 10,368 differential pairs and >550 Broadcom retimers per rack, see [cowos-packaging.md](cowos-packaging.md)). SemiAnalysis's own caveat cuts the same way as the power number: on *marketed* dense FP8 the MI455X looks competitive with VR NVL72 on TCO/PFLOP and TFLOPs/W, **but it "flags AMD has historically overstated TFLOPs and suspects low MFU"** — so effective throughput-per-watt is likely worse than the marketed figures. For this page the read is simple: **if the SemiAnalysis rack number is right, an MI455X-heavy fleet needs ~1.6x the facility power per rack of a GB300 NVL72 fleet** — a materially different power/cooling/800V provisioning problem. (SemiAnalysis "Can AMD break the CUDA Moat? AMD Advancing AI 2026", 2026-07-24; see [../AMD.md](../AMD.md), [../NVDA.md](../NVDA.md), [800v-dc-power.md](800v-dc-power.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_24_ENC_Can_AMD_break_the_CUDA_Moat_AMD_Advancing_AI_2026_email.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-06) — the energy-procurement leg is now being financed alongside the compute: Firmus's 360MW Batam cluster is paired with a 600MW firm-energy deal with Gunvor underwriting 1.2GW of new renewables + 1.5GWh of storage in South Australia by 2032 (SemiAnalysis "Nvidia GPU Debt Backstop Unleashes the AI Project Trinity", 2026-07-06).** A concrete example of the pattern this page has been tracking qualitatively — that firm power is contracted and financed as its own project leg, not procured after the fact. Firmus's **360MW AI cluster in Batam, Indonesia** (announced 2026-06-29, likely housed in a DayOne facility in Kabil Industrial Tech Park, and backed by an Nvidia backstop) sits alongside a **600MW firm-energy deal with Gunvor that will also underwrite 1.2GW of new renewables development and 1.5GWh of storage in South Australia by 2032**. Prior Firmus footprint for scale: first clusters in Singapore (H100s, immersion cooling), then an **18,000-GB300 cluster in Melbourne in a 42MW self-built datacenter, financed with a $10B facility led by Blackstone (Tactical Opportunities, Credit and Insurance) with Coatue**. SemiAnalysis's caveat is the standing constraint on this page: **Firmus "will still need to identify a datacenter provider or build their own datacenters before they can deploy GPUs"** — the energy deal does not by itself produce a powered shell. (SemiAnalysis "Nvidia GPU Debt Backstop Unleashes the AI Project Trinity", 2026-07-06; the debt/financing leg is on [hyperscaler-capex.md](hyperscaler-capex.md); Firmus has no wiki page) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_06_ENC_Nvidia_GPU_Debt_Backstop_Unleashes_the_AI_Project_Trinity_Capital_Offtake_and_Datacenters_email.html)
- **BACKFILL — HISTORICAL, dated 2025-09-03 (ingested 2026-07-24; ~11 months old, do NOT read as current) — the AWS/Anthropic counter-example to the liquid-cooled GPU norm: >1.3GW of IT capacity across three campuses in final construction for Anthropic training alone, on buildings "hyper-optimized for air-cooling" (SemiAnalysis "Amazon's AI Resurgence: AWS & Anthropic's Multi-Gigawatt Trainium Expansion", 2025-09-03).** Worth logging on this page precisely because it breaks the pattern. SemiAnalysis showed **three AWS campuses in final stages of construction "boasting over 1.3GW of IT capacity for the sole purpose of serving Anthropic's training needs,"** with **just under a million Trainium2 in the largest campus** — at the time the world's largest cluster of non-Nvidia AI chips. The building design, however, is deliberately boring: *"the design of individual building is unremarkable. Hyper-optimized for air-cooling, this blueprint is identical to 5-year-old traditional AWS Cloud datacenters."* The read for the power/cooling debate: **a custom-ASIC fleet at a lower per-chip TDP can be housed in a conventional air-cooled shell**, which is a real time-to-power and capex advantage over the direct-to-chip-liquid retrofit path — and a reminder that "GW of IT capacity" and "GW of liquid-cooled GPU capacity" are not the same base. (SemiAnalysis "Amazon's AI Resurgence: AWS & Anthropic's Multi-Gigawatt Trainium Expansion", 2025-09-03 — **historical backfill; the 1.3GW/1M-Trainium2 figures are Sep-2025 construction marks**; the Trainium silicon/economics leg is on [custom-asic-tpu.md](custom-asic-tpu.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2025_09_03_ENC_Amazons_AI_Resurgence_AWS_Anthropics_Multi-Gigawatt_Trainium_Expansion_email.html)
- **BACKFILL — HISTORICAL, dated 2025-08-20 (ingested 2026-07-24; ~11 months old) — the measured power-efficiency ladder behind "a 100MW datacenter trains 4x more tokens": GB200 at 1200W/chip vs H100 at 700W, 2.2x more power-efficient per token by Jul-2025 and a projected 4x lower Joules/token by Dec-2025 (SemiAnalysis "H100 vs GB200 NVL72 Training Benchmarks — Power, TCO and Reliability Analysis", 2025-08-20).** The cleanest measured statement on this page of *why* power-constrained operators keep refreshing silicon. Per-chip power went **up** — the **GB200 chip consumes 1200W per chip vs 700W for the H100** — yet per-token energy went sharply **down**: by **July 2025 training was 2.2x more power-efficient on a GB200 NVL72 vs an H100** (mostly FLOPS/watt, and to a lesser extent PUE gains from direct-to-chip liquid cooling where chiller use is minimised), with NVIDIA's projections implying **Joules per token 4x lower by December 2025** — so *"a datacenter with 100MW of utility power can train 4x more tokens when deploying GB200 NVL72s vs H100s."* The scale anchor SemiAnalysis uses is a good one for this page: the average US household consumed **10,791 kWh in 2022 = 1,232W of continuous average draw — a little more than the 1,200W of a single GB200 GPU.** Read: **the binding constraint is utility power, so the refresh cycle is an effective capacity expansion** — which is the mechanism behind the 2-year hardware-refresh compression this page and [tokenmaxxing.md](tokenmaxxing.md) both track. (SemiAnalysis "H100 vs GB200 NVL72 Training Benchmarks", 2025-08-20 — **historical backfill; all figures are 2024/2025 marks on H100/GB200-generation hardware, superseded by GB300/Rubin/MI455X-era numbers**) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2025_08_20_ENC_H100_vs_GB200_NVL72_Training_Benchmarks_-_Power_TCO_and_Reliability_Analysis_Software_Improvement_Over_Ti.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-22) — GE Vernova Q2'26 print: total backlog $176B (+$13B q/q), ≥125GW gas under contract by YE'26, ~20% of gas orders now data-center-driven; Electrification DC orders >$5B in H1 (>2x FY25) (GE Vernova "Q2 2026 earnings call", 2026-07-22).** [../GEV.md](../GEV.md) — a core firm-power name — delivered a second straight beat-and-raise on AI-power demand: gas GW under contract grew **100 → 116 sequentially** (shipped 3 GW while signing **20 GW** of orders + slot reservations), now **mostly sold out through 2030** with a **new ~30 GW/yr capacity target by 2030** (on top of 24 GW by '28); ~80% of gas customers traditional / **~20% data centers**. Electrification booked **$2.7B of DC orders in Q2 → >$5B in H1 (>2x all FY25)**, equipment backlog >$41B (+69% y/y). FY26 FCF guide nearly doubled to $11.5–12.5B. Read-through for this page: firm, contractable power stays the scarce binding asset, and GEV keeps pushing its sold-out horizon out rather than flagging a demand peak. [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/GEV_2Q_transcript.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-22) — MS initiates AEIS at OW ($421 PT): "no slowdown to WFE or Data Centers," confident on Meta's GW deployment (Morgan Stanley "AEIS initiation", 2026-07-22).** A light DC-power-demand read-through: MS initiates Advanced Energy (AEIS — a power-solutions provider that builds the AC/DC and HVDC power-conversion "box" inside AI-server racks) Overweight, modeling **data-center revenue +41%/+49% in 2026/27** on a proven Meta track record plus a broadening hyperscaler base (Google/Amazon/Oracle + neoclouds), with internet analyst Brian Nowak **"confident on Meta's GW deployment"** (~1.1 GW compute deployed '25 → ~1.0 GW '26). Another sell-side vote of continued conviction in data-center power capex. (Morgan Stanley "AEIS initiation", 2026-07-22)
- **NEW (2026-07-20) — MS (Byrd): BTC-miner-to-DC conversions + behind-the-meter generation as the time-to-power trade; ~38 GW US power shortfall through 2028 → ~122 GW through 2030 (Morgan Stanley · Stephen Byrd "Bitcoin-to-DC Conversions + Behind-the-Meter Generation", 2026-07-20).** Byrd sizes the **US data-center power shortfall (grid/facility-power basis) at ~38 GW through 2028, growing to ~122 GW through 2030**, and argues time-to-power providers could generate **"~$500 billion in value" through 2028 and ">$1,100 billion" through 2030**. Rates [../GEV.md](../GEV.md) / Siemens Energy / Mitsubishi Heavy OW (gas-turbine lead times 4+ yr); [../WMB.md](../WMB.md) the broadest integrated onsite/BTM model; [../BE.md](../BE.md) fuel cells at **$100-130/MWh** (AEP 1GW deal; Fremont expanding 1→5GW for $150m capex). Non-covered BTC-miner-to-AI names in the note (no wiki page — plain text): **CIFR** (OW, PT cut to $47 from $48.50), **WULF** (OW; WULF-Anthropic deal ~$19/watt net value, >4GW pipeline), **SEI/Solaris** (~$66/share standalone), **LBRT/Liberty** (Power-as-a-Service ~3GW by 2029, 1GW to Vantage), **MARA**. _(The ~38 GW-through-2028 shortfall corroborates the same figure in the MS Weaver/Salvatore note below.)_ [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Bitcoin_Miners.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-20) — Deutsche Bank (Black): Alphabet capex-per-GW $30-35bn; ~19 GW of capacity needed to serve the FY27 backlog (Deutsche Bank · Benjamin Black "Alphabet — Aiming for the Clouds", 2026-07-20).** DB pegs build cost at **$30-35bn per GW** and models [../GOOG.md](../GOOG.md) needing **~19 GW of capacity to serve its FY27 backlog (Scenario B)**; rented capacity from SpaceX (~110k GPUs) is modeled at 0. A useful GOOG-specific $/GW mark alongside the BofA (~$37bn/GW Google) and JPM (~$35bn/GW) benchmarks already on this page.
